I guess mostly my sleep. It’s been going on a long time now… I fall into bed just exhausted, like I should be out cold, but then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater clicks, someone closes a door, whatever, and I’m awake again.

And then I’m up before the alarm half the time, just lying there, and it feels like I never really slept. I mean, I work in home health, so I’m used to being tired—people think that’s just part of it, and maybe it is sometimes—but this feels like I’m not ever getting real rest.

I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.

So… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.

I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.

I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF51.01, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess mostly my sleep. It’s been going on a long time now… I fall into bed just exhausted, like I should be out cold, but then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater clicks, someone closes a door, whatever, and I’m awake again.\n\nAnd then I’m up before the alarm half the time, just lying there, and it feels like I never really slept. I mean, I work in home health, so I’m used to being tired—people think that’s just part of it, and maybe it is sometimes—but this feels like I’m not ever getting real rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worrying exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure… everybody does, right? Work stuff, errands, whether I remembered to pick up milk, and then my daughter will text me something and I get sidetracked. \n\nBut I don’t know if it’s, like, worrying in the way you mean. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I mean, work can get hectic, especially going from one house to another, and traffic is ridiculous lately—there’s this one intersection I swear they’ve been fixing forever.\n\nBut anxious? I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.

Every little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.

But the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.

I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. Sometimes I’m just exhausted and I get into bed thinking I should be out like a light, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, I think about things, sure… everybody does, right? Work stuff, errands, whether I remembered to pick up milk, and then my daughter will text me something and I get sidetracked. \n\nBut I don’t know if it’s, like, worrying in the way you mean. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I mean, work can get hectic, especially going from one house to another, and traffic is ridiculous lately—there’s this one intersection I swear they’ve been fixing forever.\n\nBut anxious? I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. Sometimes I’m just exhausted and I get into bed thinking I should be out like a light, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your waking up at night is more because of your thoughts or because of the noises around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the noises, honestly. I’ll fall into bed just wiped out, and then the smallest thing wakes me up—the house settling, heat coming on, somebody outside, whatever. It’s like I never get into a deep sleep.\n\nThen once I’m awake, yeah, my thoughts start going a little, like about the alarm or how tired I’m going to be, but I don’t think the thoughts are what wake me up first. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I mean, everybody worries, right? Bills, work, getting to someone’s house on time… my cousin says she sleeps better if she writes things down, and I tried that once, but then I just ended up making a grocery list.

But no, really, my sleep has been broken for a long time. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.

I mean, I work in home health, so I’m used to being tired—people think that’s just part of it, and maybe it is sometimes—but this feels like I’m not ever getting real rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worrying exactly. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your waking up at night is more because of your thoughts or because of the noises around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the noises, honestly. I’ll fall into bed just wiped out, and then the smallest thing wakes me up—the house settling, heat coming on, somebody outside, whatever. It’s like I never get into a deep sleep.\n\nThen once I’m awake, yeah, my thoughts start going a little, like about the alarm or how tired I’m going to be, but I don’t think the thoughts are what wake me up first. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would improve if you weren't worried about things?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I mean, everybody worries, right? Bills, work, getting to someone’s house on time… my cousin says she sleeps better if she writes things down, and I tried that once, but then I just ended up making a grocery list.\n\nBut no, really, my sleep has been broken for a long time. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your sleep would be better if you didn't have any worries?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / home_health_aide / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because for a long time now my sleep has been broken—I fall into bed exhausted, wake up to every little sound in the house, and then I'm up before the alarm feeling like I never really rested.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, I guess, but I really don’t think worries are the whole problem. Like, I’ll go to bed completely exhausted, thinking I should just sleep, and then the smallest noise in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, whatever.

And then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, just feeling like I didn’t really sleep. It’s been like that for a long time. I mean, I’ve tried to tell myself not to think about things at night, but then I’m still waking up. So… no, I don’t think no worries would magically fix it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess mostly my sleep. I mean, I work in home health, so I’m used to being tired—people think that’s just part of it, and maybe it is sometimes—but this feels like I’m not ever getting real rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worrying exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure… everybody does, right? Work stuff, errands, whether I remembered to pick up milk, and then my daughter will text me something and I get sidetracked. \n\nBut I don’t know if it’s, like, worrying in the way you mean. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I mean, work can get hectic, especially going from one house to another, and traffic is ridiculous lately—there’s this one intersection I swear they’ve been fixing forever.\n\nBut anxious? I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would improve if you weren't worried about things?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I mean, everybody worries, right? Bills, work, getting to someone’s house on time… my cousin says she sleeps better if she writes things down, and I tried that once, but then I just ended up making a grocery list.\n\nBut no, really, my sleep has been broken for a long time. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would be better if you didn't have any worries?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, but I really don’t think worries are the whole problem. Like, I’ll go to bed completely exhausted, thinking I should just sleep, and then the smallest noise in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, whatever.\n\nAnd then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, just feeling like I didn’t really sleep. It’s been like that for a long time. I mean, I’ve tried to tell myself not to think about things at night, but then I’m still waking up. So… no, I don’t think no worries would magically fix it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your sleep problems would completely go away if you could stop worrying about things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it would completely go away. I mean, maybe I’d be a little calmer, sure, but the sleep thing feels bigger than just worrying.

I come home so tired—like after being in and out of people’s houses all day, and you know how some houses have those little creaks and machines beeping, I guess I notice every sound now—and then at my own house it’s the same. I fall into bed exhausted, and then some tiny noise wakes me up. The heat, a floorboard, something outside.

And then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I didn’t really rest. So… no, stopping worry wouldn’t make it just disappear.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess mostly my sleep. It’s been going on a long time now… I fall into bed just exhausted, like I should be out cold, but then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater clicks, someone closes a door, whatever, and I’m awake again.\n\nAnd then I’m up before the alarm half the time, just lying there, and it feels like I never really slept. I mean, I work in home health, so I’m used to being tired—people think that’s just part of it, and maybe it is sometimes—but this feels like I’m not ever getting real rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it worrying exactly. I mean, I think about things, sure… everybody does, right? Work stuff, errands, whether I remembered to pick up milk, and then my daughter will text me something and I get sidetracked. \n\nBut I don’t know if it’s, like, worrying in the way you mean. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I mean, work can get hectic, especially going from one house to another, and traffic is ridiculous lately—there’s this one intersection I swear they’ve been fixing forever.\n\nBut anxious? I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. Sometimes I’m just exhausted and I get into bed thinking I should be out like a light, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your waking up at night is more because of your thoughts or because of the noises around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the noises, honestly. I’ll fall into bed just wiped out, and then the smallest thing wakes me up—the house settling, heat coming on, somebody outside, whatever. It’s like I never get into a deep sleep.\n\nThen once I’m awake, yeah, my thoughts start going a little, like about the alarm or how tired I’m going to be, but I don’t think the thoughts are what wake me up first. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would improve if you weren't worried about things?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would be better if you didn't have any worries?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, but I really don’t think worries are the whole problem. I fall into bed exhausted, and then some tiny noise wakes me up. The heat, a floorboard, something outside.\n\nAnd then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I didn’t really rest. During the day I mostly just try to get through what’s in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “on edge” makes me think of somebody jumping at everything, and I’m not sure that’s me during the day. I mean, work can get hectic, especially going from one house to another, and traffic is ridiculous lately—there’s this one intersection I swear they’ve been fixing forever.\n\nBut anxious? I don’t really know. I mostly just keep moving and do what I’m supposed to do. I haven’t really stopped to think about whether I feel on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean… I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m anxious,” if that’s what you mean. I just kind of go from one thing to the next.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get to a client’s house and the GPS kept rerouting me, and then I remembered I hadn’t called my sister back—she always calls at the worst times, not on purpose, but still—and I was irritated, sure. But anxious? I’m not sure. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself preoccupied with thoughts about the future or things that might go wrong?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’m preoccupied with it. I mean, the future feels like such a big word, you know? I think about things I need to handle—bills, schedules, whether I’m going to be late to someone’s house—but then I’ll also start thinking about, like, whether I left laundry in the washer, and then I remember my neighbor’s dog was barking half the night.\n\nSo… maybe I think about stuff, but I don’t know if I’m sitting there imagining everything going wrong. I haven’t really paid attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really uncomfortable or stressed, like if I’m running late or something unexpected happens, but “intense fear” sounds… I don’t know, like something dramatic.\n\nThere was this one time in the grocery store where the line was so long and the cashier was new, and I remember thinking I should’ve gone to the other store—the one by my sister’s place, though their parking lot is terrible—anyway, I was frustrated and wanted to leave. But I don’t know if it peaked in minutes or whatever. I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you worry more than most people about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how much other people worry, honestly. People don’t exactly tell you everything that’s going through their head, you know? Like I see clients and they’ll seem calm, but then they’ll mention they’ve been up since five thinking about their son or their prescription refill or something.\n\nI mean, I think about regular stuff—getting to work on time, whether I remembered to pick something up, bills, family things—but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It just feels like… life stuff, I guess. Maybe I’m used to it, so it doesn’t stand out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying about everyday things is excessive?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, excessive compared to what? Some days it feels like I’m just keeping track of regular things—work, groceries, whether I said I’d call someone back. And then I’ll remember something small, like I need paper towels, and that turns into thinking about the store being crowded, and then I wonder if I should’ve gone yesterday.\n\nBut I don’t know… I haven’t really measured it, I guess. It just feels like the normal amount of stuff a person has to think about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do get things stuck in my head sometimes, like if I forgot to do something or I said something awkward, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nLike yesterday I kept thinking I needed to stop for milk, and then I got there and remembered the eggs too, and the store had moved things around again—why do they always do that? It makes the whole trip take longer. But thoughts repeating like I can’t stop them… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that you try to push away or stop thinking about, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’ll try not to think about something annoying, like if I forgot to return a call or if I bought the wrong thing at the store, and then it’ll pop back up while I’m doing dishes or driving.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. Sometimes I think the brain is just… messy. Like I’ll be helping someone with breakfast and suddenly remember I never switched the laundry, and then I’m thinking about detergent prices, which have gotten ridiculous. So, I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. Sometimes I’m just exhausted and I get into bed thinking I should be out like a light, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your waking up at night is more because of your thoughts or because of the noises around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the noises, honestly. I’ll fall into bed just wiped out, and then the smallest thing wakes me up—the house settling, heat coming on, somebody outside, whatever. It’s like I never get into a deep sleep.\n\nThen once I’m awake, yeah, my thoughts start going a little, like about the alarm or how tired I’m going to be, but I don’t think the thoughts are what wake me up first. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would improve if you weren't worried about things?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I mean, everybody worries, right? Bills, work, getting to someone’s house on time… my cousin says she sleeps better if she writes things down, and I tried that once, but then I just ended up making a grocery list.\n\nBut no, really, my sleep has been broken for a long time. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would be better if you didn't have any worries?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, but I really don’t think worries are the whole problem. Like, I’ll go to bed completely exhausted, thinking I should just sleep, and then the smallest noise in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, whatever.\n\nAnd then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, just feeling like I didn’t really sleep. It’s been like that for a long time. I mean, I’ve tried to tell myself not to think about things at night, but then I’m still waking up. So… no, I don’t think no worries would magically fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would completely go away if you could stop worrying about things?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. I mean, maybe I’d be a little calmer, sure, but the sleep thing feels bigger than just worrying.\n\nI come home so tired—like after being in and out of people’s houses all day, and you know how some houses have those little creaks and machines beeping, I guess I notice every sound now—and then at my own house it’s the same. I fall into bed exhausted, and then some tiny noise wakes me up. The heat, a floorboard, something outside.\n\nAnd then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I didn’t really rest. So… no, stopping worry wouldn’t make it just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems are more related to your anxiety or to the noises and sounds that wake you up?\n\nPatient: I guess… it feels more like the sounds wake me up, if I had to pick. I mean, I’m tired when I get into bed—really tired—and then it’s like every little thing in the house gets through to me. The heater clicking, a car outside, whatever small noise.\n\nThough I don’t know, maybe being wound up makes me notice it more. My neighbor’s dog used to bark at nothing, and I’d lie there thinking, “How does anybody sleep through this?” But even when it’s not that, I’m still up before the alarm feeling like I never really rested. So I wouldn’t say it’s just anxiety. It’s been broken like this for a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it would help some, probably. If it was completely quiet, maybe I wouldn’t wake up quite as much… but I don’t know if it would fix it.

I mean, I’m here because this has been going on a long time now. I fall into bed just exhausted, and then every little sound in the house seems to wake me up—the heater, something settling, a car outside. It’s funny, because in other people’s homes for work I notice sounds too, like clocks ticking or refrigerators humming, and then I come home and it’s like I can’t shut that off.

But even when it’s quieter, I’m still up before the alarm a lot of mornings, feeling like I didn’t really rest. So… yes, quiet might make it better, but I’m not sure it would make me sleep normally.

I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming back in a way that feels unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel compelled to think about over and over, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not compelled, exactly. I mean, I’ll go over things in my head, but it’s usually more like regular reminders or second-guessing myself.\n\nLike if I’m on the way to someone’s house for work, I might think, did I bring the gloves, did I sign the sheet last time, and then I’m thinking about traffic or whether I should stop for gas because it’s cheaper near the highway—well, not that cheap anymore. But I don’t know that I feel forced to think about something over and over. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes it’s worries, sure, but mostly my sleep is just broken. I’ll be so tired I feel like I could sleep standing up, and then I get into bed and it’s like I’m half-listening to the whole house all night.\n\nEvery little sound wakes me up—the heat clicking, a car outside, somebody shifting around—and then I’m awake before the alarm anyway. And then you start thinking, well, now I’m going to be dragging tomorrow, and I have to be at someone’s place early, and if traffic is bad… you know how it goes.\n\nBut the main thing is I don’t feel rested. I can be in bed for hours and still wake up feeling like I never really slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually lie awake at night because you're thinking about things that worry you or make you anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not always because I’m anxious, exactly. Sometimes I’m just exhausted and I get into bed thinking I should be out like a light, and then every little sound in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, someone moving around—my neighbor has this truck that seems louder at night for some reason.\n\nAnd then once I’m awake, I’ll start thinking, sure, like, “Great, now I’m going to be tired before the alarm even goes off.” But the bigger thing is my sleep’s just been broken for a long time. I wake up before the alarm and feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your waking up at night is more because of your thoughts or because of the noises around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the noises, honestly. I’ll fall into bed just wiped out, and then the smallest thing wakes me up—the house settling, heat coming on, somebody outside, whatever. It’s like I never get into a deep sleep.\n\nThen once I’m awake, yeah, my thoughts start going a little, like about the alarm or how tired I’m going to be, but I don’t think the thoughts are what wake me up first. It’s been this broken sleep for a long time, and I’m usually up before the alarm feeling like I didn’t really rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems would improve if you weren't worried about things?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I don’t think that’s the main thing. I mean, everybody worries, right? Bills, work, getting to someone’s house on time… my cousin says she sleeps better if she writes things down, and I tried that once, but then I just ended up making a grocery list.\n\nBut no, really, my sleep has been broken for a long time. I fall into bed exhausted, and then every tiny sound in the house wakes me up. Then I’m up before the alarm anyway, feeling like I never really rested. So if I worried less, maybe it would help some, but I don’t think it would fix the sleep.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would be better if you didn't have any worries?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, but I really don’t think worries are the whole problem. Like, I’ll go to bed completely exhausted, thinking I should just sleep, and then the smallest noise in the house wakes me up. The heater, a car outside, whatever.\n\nAnd then I’m awake before the alarm anyway, just feeling like I didn’t really sleep. It’s been like that for a long time. I mean, maybe I’d be a little calmer, sure, but the sleep thing feels bigger than just worrying.\n\nI come home so tired—like after being in and out of people’s houses all day, and you know how some houses have those little creaks and machines beeping, I guess I notice every sound now—and then at my own house it’s the same.
